Noise Covariance Estimation in Multi-Task High-dimensional Linear Models
Kai Tan, Gabriel Romon, Pierre C Bellec

Abstract
This paper studies the multi-task high-dimensional linear regression models where the noise among different tasks is correlated, in the moderately high dimensional regime where sample size n n and dimension p p are of the same order. Our goal is to estimate the covariance matrix of the noise random vectors, or equivalently the correlation of the noise variables on any pair of two tasks. Treating the regression coefficients as a nuisance parameter, we leverage the multi-task elastic-net and multi-task lasso estimators to estimate the nuisance. By precisely understanding the bias of the squared residual matrix and by correcting this bias, we develop a novel estimator of the noise covariance that converges in Frobenius norm at the rate n − 1 / 2 n^{-1/2} when the covariates are Gaussian. This novel estimator is efficiently computable. Under suitable conditions, the proposed estimator of the noise covariance attains the same rate of convergence as the “oracle” estimator that knows in advance the regression coefficients of the multi-task model. The Frobenius error bounds obtained in this paper also illustrate the advantage of this new estimator compared to a method-of-moments estimator that does not attempt to estimate the nuisance. As a byproduct of our techniques, we obtain an estimate of the generalization error of the multi-task elastic-net and multi-task lasso estimators. Extensive simulation studies are carried out to illustrate the numerical performance of the proposed method.



1
Introduction

1.1
Model and estimation target

Consider a multi-task linear model with
T
tasks and
n
i.i.d. observations
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},Y_{i1},Y_{i2},\dots,Y_{iT})
,
\forall i=1,...,n
, where
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is a random feature vector and
Y_{i1},\ldots,Y_{iT}
are responses
in the model

\displaystyle Y_{it}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+E_{it}

\displaystyle\text{for each }t=1,...,T;i=1,...,n

\displaystyle\text{(scalar form)},

(1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\smash{{}^{(t)}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\smash{{}^{(t)}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\smash{{}^{(t)}}

\displaystyle\text{for each }t=1,...,T

\displaystyle\text{(vector form)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle\text{(matrix form)},

where
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is the design matrix with rows
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top})_{i=1,...,n}
,

\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}=(Y_{1t},...,Y_{nt})^{\top}
is the response vector for task
t
,

{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(t)}=(E_{1t},...,E_{nt})^{\top}
is the noise vector for task
t
,

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is an unknown fixed coefficient vector for task
t
.
In matrix form,

\boldsymbol{Y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
is the response matrix with columns
\boldsymbol{y}^{{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{y}^{(T)}
,

\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
has columns
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(1)}},...,{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(T)}
,
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}
is an unknown coefficient matrix with
columns
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T)}
. The three forms in (
1
) are equivalent.

While the
n
vectors
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top},y_{i}^{(1)},\ldots,y_{i}^{(T)})_{i=1,...,n}
of dimension
p+T
are i.i.d.,
we assume that for each observation
i=1,...,n
, the noise random variables
E_{i1},...,E_{iT}
are centered and correlated.

The focus of the present paper is on estimation of the
noise covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
, which has entries

\boldsymbol{S}_{tt^{\prime}}={\mathbb{E}}[\varepsilon_{1}^{(t)}\varepsilon_{1}^{(t^{\prime})}]
for any pair
t,t^{\prime}=1,\ldots,T
, or equivalently

\boldsymbol{S}={\mathbb{E}}[\tfrac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}].

The noise covariance plays a crucial role in multi-task linear models because it characterizes the noise level and correlation between different tasks:
if tasks
t=1,...,T
represent time this captures temporal correlation;
if tasks
t=1,...,T
represent different activation areas in the brain (e.g.,
[
11
]
) this captures spatial correlation.

Since
\boldsymbol{S}
is the estimation target,
we view
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
as an unknown nuisance parameter.
If
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}=\mathbf{0}
, then
\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{E}
, hence
\boldsymbol{E}
is directly observed and a natural estimator is the sample covariance

\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
.
There are other possible choices for the sample covariance;
ours coincides with the maximum likelihood estimator of the centered Gaussian model
where the
n
samples are i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bf 0,\boldsymbol{S})
.
In the presence of a nuisance parameter
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\neq\bf 0
,
the above sample covariance is not computable since we only observe
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})
and do not have access to
\boldsymbol{E}
.
Thus we will refer to

\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}

as the
oracle estimator
for
\boldsymbol{S}
, and its error

\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{S}

will serve as a benchmark.

The nuisance parameter
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is not of interest by itself, but if an estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is available that provides good estimation
of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, we would hope to leverage
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
to estimate the nuisance
and improve estimation of
\boldsymbol{S}
.
For instance given an estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n\to 0
, one may use the estimator

\textstyle{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{(\text{naive})}=\frac{1}{n}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})

(2)

to consistently estimate
\boldsymbol{S}
in Frobenius norm.
We refer to this estimator as the
naive estimator
since it is obtained by simply replacing the noise
\boldsymbol{E}
in the oracle estimator
\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
with the residual matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
.
However,
in the regime
p/n\to\gamma
of interest in the present paper,
the convergence
\lVert\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n\to 0
is not true even for
T=1

and common high-dimensional estimators such as Ridge regression
[
19
]
or the Lasso
[
1
,
29
]
.
Simulations in
Section

4
will show that
(
2
) presents a major bias for estimation of
\boldsymbol{S}
.
One goal of this paper is to develop estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
of
\boldsymbol{S}

by exploiting a commonly used estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
of the nuisance,
so that in the regime
p/n\to\gamma
the error
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}
is comparable to the benchmark
\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{S}
.

1.2
Related literature

If
T=1
, the above model (
1
) reduces to the standard linear model
with
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
and response vector
\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
We will refer to the
T=1
case as the single-task linear model and drop the superscript
(1)
for brevity, i.e.,

y_{i}=\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\varepsilon_{i}
,
where
\varepsilon_{i}
are i.i.d. with mean
0
, and unknown variance
\sigma^{2}
. The coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
is typically assumed to be
s
-sparse, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
has at most
s
nonzero entries. In this single-task linear model, estimation of noise covariance
\boldsymbol{S}
reduces to estimation of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}={\mathbb{E}}[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}]
, which has been studied in the literature.
Fan et al. 2012
proposed a consistent estimator for
\sigma^{2}
based on a refitted cross validation method, which assumes the support of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
is correctly recovered;
[
9
]
and
[
35
]
introduced square-root Lasso (scaled Lasso) to jointly estimate the coefficient
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
and noise variance
\sigma^{2}
by

\textstyle(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{},\widehat{\sigma})=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\sigma>0}\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\rVert^{2}}{2n\sigma}+\frac{\sigma}{2}+\lambda_{0}\lVert\boldsymbol{\beta}\rVert_{1}.

(3)

This estimator
\widehat{\sigma}
is consistent only when the prediction error
\lVert\boldsymbol{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\rVert^{2}/n
goes to 0, which requires
s\log(p)/n\to 0
.
Estimation of
\sigma^{2}
without assumption on
\boldsymbol{X}
was proposed in
[
37
]
by utilizing natural parameterization of the penalized likelihood of the linear model. Their estimator can be expressed as the minimizer of the Lasso problem:

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\lambda}=\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\frac{1}{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\rVert^{2}+2\lambda\lVert\boldsymbol{\beta}\rVert_{1}.
Consistency of these estimators
[
35
,
9
,
10
,
37
]
requires
s\log(p)/n\to 0
and does not hold in the high-dimensional proportional regime
p/n\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
.
For this proportional regime
p/n\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
,
[
17
]
introduced a method-of-moments estimator
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
of
\sigma^{2}
,

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{n+p+1}{n(n+1)}\lVert\boldsymbol{y}\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}\rVert^{2},

(4)

which is unbiased, consistent, and asymptotically normal in high-dimensional linear models with Gaussian predictors and errors. Moreover,
[
24
]
developed an EigenPrism procedure for the same task as well as confidence intervals for
\sigma^{2}
.
The estimation procedures in these two papers don’t attempt to estimate the nuisance parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
, and require no sparsity on
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
and isometry structure on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, but assume
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}
is bounded.
Maximum Likelihood Estimators (MLEs) were studied in

[
18
]
for joint estimation of noise level and signal strength in high-dimensional linear models with fixed effects; they showed that a classical MLE for random-effects models may also be used effectively in fixed-effects models.

In the proportional regime,

[
2
,
29
]
used the Lasso to estimate the nuisance
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
and produce estimator for
\sigma^{2}
. Their approach requires an uncorrelated Gaussian design assumption with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
.

Bellec 2020
provided consistent estimators of a similar nature for
\sigma^{2}
using more general M-estimators with convex penalty without requiring
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
. In the special case of the squared loss, this estimator has the form
[
2
,
29
,
4
]

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=(n-\hat{\mathsf{df}})^{-2}\big\{\lVert\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}\rVert^{2}(n+p-2\hat{\mathsf{df}})-\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{})\rVert\big\},

(5)

where
\hat{\mathsf{df}}=\trace[(\partial/\partial\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}]
denotes the degrees of freedom. This estimator coincides with the method-of-moments estimator in
[
17
]
when
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}=\bf 0
.

For multi-task high-dimensional linear model (
1
) with
T\geq 2
, the estimation of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is studied in
[
28
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
33
]
. These works suggest to use a joint convex optimization problem over the tasks to estimate
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
. A popular choice is the multi-task elastic-net, which
solves the convex optimization problem

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\Big(\frac{1}{2n}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\lambda\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{2,1}+\frac{\tau}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\Big),

(6)

where
\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|{\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}\|_{2}
, and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\rm{F}}
denotes the Frobenius norm of a matrix.
This optimization problem can be efficiently solved by existing statistical packages, for instance, scikit-learn
[
32
]
, and glmnet
[
23
]
.
Note that (
6
) is also referred to as multi-task (group) Lasso and multi-task Ridge if
\tau=0
and
\lambda=0
, respectively.

van de Geer and Stucky 2016
extended square-root Lasso
[
9
]
and scaled Lasso
[
35
]
to multi-task setting by solving the following problem

\displaystyle({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}},{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}})=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{S}\succ 0}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\trace\big((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B})\boldsymbol{S}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B})^{\top}\big)+\trace(\boldsymbol{S}^{\frac{1}{2}})+2\lambda_{0}\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{1}\Big\},

(7)

where
\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{j,t}|B_{jt}|
. Note that the covariance estimator in (
7
) is constrained to be positive definite.

Molstad 2019
studied the same problem and proposed to estimate
\boldsymbol{S}
by (
2
) with
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
7
), which is consistent under Frobenius norm loss when
\lVert\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n\to 0
.
In a recent paper,
Bellec and Romon 2021
studied the multi-task Lasso problem and proposed confidence intervals for single entries of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and confidence ellipsoids for single rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
under the assumption that
\boldsymbol{S}
is proportional to the identity, which may be restrictive in practice. This literature generalizes degrees of freedom adjustments from single-task to multi-task models, which we will illustrate in
Section

2
.

Noise covariance estimation in the high dimensional multi-task linear model is a difficult problem. If the estimand
\boldsymbol{S}
is known to be diagonal,
estimating
\boldsymbol{S}
reduces to the estimation of noise variance for each task,
in which the existing methods for single-task high-dimensional linear models can be applied.
Nonetheless, for general positive semi-definite matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
, the noise among different tasks may be correlated,
hence the existing methods are not readily applicable, and a more careful analysis is called for to incorporate the correlation between different tasks.
Fourdrinier et al. 2021
considered estimating
\boldsymbol{S}
for the multi-task model (
1
) where rows of
\boldsymbol{E}
have elliptically symmetric distribution and in the classical regime
p\leq n
. However, their estimator has no statistical guarantee under Frobenius norm loss.

Recently, for the proportional regime
p/n\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
,
[
15
]
generalized the estimator
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
in
[
2
]
to the multi-task setting with
T=2
. Their work covers correlated Gaussian designs, where a Lasso or Ridge regression is used to estimate
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
for the first task, and another Lasso or Ridge regression is used to estimate
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(2)}
for the second task.
In other words, they estimate the coefficient vector for each task separately instead of using a multi-task estimator like (
6
).
It is not trivial to adapt their estimator from the setting
T=2
to larger
T
, and allow
T
to increase with
n
.
This present paper takes a different route and aims to fill this gap by proposing a novel noise covariance estimator with theoretical guarantees.
Of course, our method applies directly to the 2-task linear model considered in
[
15
]
.

1.3
Main Contributions

The present paper introduces a novel estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
in (
11
) of the noise covariance
\boldsymbol{S}
, which provides consistent estimation of
\boldsymbol{S}
in Frobenius norm,
in the regime where
p
and
n
are of the same order.
The estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
is based on the multi-task elastic-net
estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
6
) of the nuisance, and can be seen
as a de-biased version of the naive estimator (
2
).
The naive estimator (
2
) suffers from a strong bias in the regime
where
p
and
n
are of the same order,
and the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}

is constructed by precisely understanding this bias and correcting it.

After introducing this novel estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
in
Definition

2.2
below,
we prove several rates of convergence for the Frobenius error

\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
, which is comparable, in terms of rate of convergence,
to the benchmark
\lVert\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
under suitable assumptions.

As a by-product of the techniques developed for the construction of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
,
we obtain estimates of the generalization error
of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
, which are of independent interest and can be used for parameter tuning.

1.4
Notation

Basic notation and definitions that will be used in the rest of the paper are given here. Let
[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The vectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
denote the canonical basis vector of the corresponding index.
We consider restrictions of vectors (resp., of matrices)
by zeroing the corresponding entries (resp., columns).
More precisely, for
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and index set
B\subset[p]
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the vector
with
(\boldsymbol{v}_{B})_{j}=0
if
j\notin B
and
(\boldsymbol{v}_{B})_{j}=v_{j}
if
j\in B
.
If
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
and
B\subset[p]
,
\boldsymbol{X}_{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}

is such that
(\boldsymbol{X}_{B})\boldsymbol{e}_{j}={\mathbf{0}}
if
j\notin B
and

(\boldsymbol{X}_{B})\boldsymbol{e}_{j}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
if
j\in B
.
For a real vector
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{a}\right\rVert
denotes its Euclidean norm. For any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
,
\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}
is its Moore–Penrose inverse;
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{A}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{A}\right\rVert_{\rm{op}}
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{A}\right\rVert_{*}
denote its Frobenius, operator and nuclear norm, respectively.
Let
\lVert\boldsymbol{A}\rVert_{0}
be the number of non-zero rows of
\boldsymbol{A}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{A}\otimes\boldsymbol{B}
be the Kronecker product of
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
, and
\langle\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\rangle=\trace(\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B})

is the Frobenius inner product for matrices of identical size.
For
\boldsymbol{A}
symmetric,
\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})
and
\phi_{\max}(\boldsymbol{A})
denote its smallest and largest eigenvalues, respectively.
Let
\boldsymbol{I}_{n}
denote the identity matrix of size
n
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
For a random sequence
\xi_{n}
, we write
\xi_{n}=O_{P}(a_{n})
if
\xi_{n}/a_{n}
is stochastically bounded.

C
denotes an absolute constant and

C(\tau,\gamma)
stands for a generic positive constant depending on
\tau,\gamma
;
their expression may vary from place to place.

1.5
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.

Section

2
introduces our proposed estimator for noise covariance.

Section

3
presents our main theoretical results on proposed estimator and some relevant estimators.

Section

4
demonstrates through numerical experiments that our estimator outperforms several existing methods in the literature, which corroborates our theoretical findings in
Section

3
.

Section

5
provides discussion and points out some future research directions.
Proofs of all the results stated in the main body are given in the supplementary, which starts with an outline for ease of navigation.

2
Estimating noise covariance, with possibly diverging number of tasks
\boldsymbol{T}

Before we can define our noise covariance estimator, we need to introduce the following building blocks.
Let
\hat{{S}}=\{k\in[p]:{{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}}\neq 0\}

denote the set of nonzero rows of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
6
), and let
|\hat{{S}}|
denote the cardinality of
\hat{{S}}
.
For each
k\in\hat{{S}}
, define
\boldsymbol{H}^{(k)}=\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\|^{-1}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}~\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\|^{-2})
, which is the Hessian of the map
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\lambda\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert
at
\boldsymbol{u}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
when
\boldsymbol{u}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
Define
\boldsymbol{M},\boldsymbol{M}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{pT\times pT}
by

\textstyle\boldsymbol{M}_{1}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{{S}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{{S}}}+\tau n\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{{S}}}),\qquad\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}_{1}+n\sum_{k\in\hat{{S}}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{(k)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\top})

(8)

where
\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{{S}}}=\sum_{k\in\hat{{S}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times p}
.
Define the residual matrix
\boldsymbol{F}
, the error matrix
\boldsymbol{H}
, and
\boldsymbol{N}
by

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}},\qquad\boldsymbol{H}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}),\qquad\boldsymbol{N}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}^{\top})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{Tn\times Tn}.

(9)

To construct our estimator we also make use of the so-called interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
5
]
)
.

The
interaction matrix

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
of the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
6
) is defined by

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top})\boldsymbol{N}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{i}).

(10)

The matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
was introduced in
[
5
]
, where it is used alongside the multi-task Lasso estimator (
\tau=0
in (
6
)).
It generalizes the degrees of freedom from
Stein 1981
to the multi-task case.
Intuitively, it captures the correlation between the residuals on different tasks
[
5
, Lemma F.1]
.
Our definition of the noise covariance estimator involves
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
,
although our statistical purposes differ greatly from the confidence intervals developed in
[
5
]
.

We are now ready to introduce our estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
of the noise covariance
\boldsymbol{S}
.

Definition 2.2

(Noise covariance estimator)
.

With
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
as above, define

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}=(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})^{-1}\Bigl[\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\big((p+n)\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\big)\boldsymbol{F}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\Bigr](n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})^{-1}.

(11)

Efficient solvers (e.g.,
sklearn.linear_model.MultiTaskElasticNet
in
[
32
]
) are available to compute
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
.
Computation of
\boldsymbol{F}
is then straightforward, and computing the matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}

only requires inverting a matrix of size
|\hat{{S}}|

[
5
, Section 5]
.
The estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
generalizes the scalar estimator
(
5
) to the multi-task setting
in the sense that for
T=1
,
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
is exactly equal to (
5
).
Note that unlike in (
5
), here
\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}
,
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
and
(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})
are matrices of size
T\times T
: the order of matrix multiplication in
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
matters and should not be switched.
This non-commutativity is not present for
T=1
in (
5
) where matrices in
{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
are reduced to scalars.
Another special case of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
can be seen in
[
15
]
for
T=2
where the matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}
is diagonal and the two columns of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times 2}
are two Lasso or Ridge estimators computed independently of each other, one for each task. Except in these two special cases — (
5
) for
T=1
,
[
15
]
for
T=2
and two Lasso/Ridge — we are not aware of previously proposed estimators of the same form as
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
.

3
Theoretical analysis

3.1
Oracle and method-of-moments estimator

Before moving on to the theoretical analysis of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
, we state our randomness assumptions for
\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{X}
and we study two preliminary estimators:
the oracle
\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
and another estimator obtained by the method of moments.

Assumption 1

(Gaussian noise)
.

\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
is a Gaussian noise matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bf 0,\boldsymbol{S})
rows, where
\boldsymbol{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
is an unknown positive semi-definite matrix.

An oracle with access to the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{E}
may compute the oracle estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(oracle)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
, with convergence rate given by the following theorem, which will serve as a benchmark.

Proposition 3.1

(Convergence rate of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\text{(oracle)}}
)
.

Under
1
,

{\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(oracle)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\big]=\tfrac{1}{n}[(\trace(\boldsymbol{S}))^{2}+\trace(\boldsymbol{S}^{2})].

(12)

Consequently,
n^{-1}(\trace(\boldsymbol{S}))^{2}\leq{\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(oracle)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\big]\leq 2n^{-1}(\trace(\boldsymbol{S}))^{2}
.

The next assumption concerns the design matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
with rows
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{n}^{\top}
.

Assumption 2

(Gaussian design)
.

\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is a Gaussian design matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}_{p}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
rows, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a known positive definite matrix. The matrices
\boldsymbol{E}
and
\boldsymbol{X}
are independent.

Under the preceding assumptions, we obtain the following method-of-moments estimator, which extends the estimator for noise variance in
[
17
]
to the multi-task setting. Its error will also serve as a benchmark.

Proposition 3.2
.

Under
1
and
2
, the method-of-moments estimator defined as

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}=\frac{(n+1+p)}{n(n+1)}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}-\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}

(13)

is unbiased for
\boldsymbol{S}
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{E}}[{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}]=\boldsymbol{S}.
Furthermore, the Frobenius error is bounded from below as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\geq\frac{p-2}{(n+1)^{2}}\big[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\big]^{2}.

(14)

By (
14
), a larger norm
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
induces a larger variance for
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
.
Our goal with an estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
, when a good estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
of the nuisance is available, is to improve upon the right-hand side of (
14
)
when the estimation error
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}
is smaller than

\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
.

A high-probability upper bound of the form

\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq C\frac{n+p}{n^{2}}[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]^{2}
,
that matches the lower bound (
14
) when
p>n
,
is a consequence of our main result below.
Indeed, when
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}=\bf 0
then
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}=\bf 0
and our estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
from
Definition

2.2

coincides with
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
up to the minor modification
of replacing
n+1
by
n
in (
13
).
This replacement is immaterial
compared to the right-hand side in (
14
).
Furthermore, such
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}

corresponds to one of
\tau
or
\lambda
being
+\infty
in (
6
)
and the aforementioned upper bound
follows by taking
\tau=+\infty
in the proof of
Theorem

3.3
below.
The empirical results in
Section

4
confirm that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
has smaller variance compared to
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
in simulations.

3.2
Theoretical results for proposed estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}

We have established lower bounds for the oracle estimator and the
method-of-moments estimator that will serve as benchmarks.
We turn to the analysis of the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
from
Definition

2.2

under the following additional assumptions.

Assumption 3

(High-dimensional regime)
.

n,p
satisfy
p/n\leq\gamma
for a constant
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
.

For asymptotic statements such as those involving the stochastically
bounded notation
O_{p}(\cdot)
or the convergence in probability in (
18
) below, we implicitly consider a sequence
of multi-task problems indexed by
n
where
p,T,\boldsymbol{B}^{*},{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}},\boldsymbol{S}
all implicitly
depend on
n
. The Assumptions, such as
p/n\leq\gamma
above, are required to hold at
all points of the sequence. In particular,
p/n\to\gamma^{\prime}
is allowed for any limit
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma
under
3
, although our results do not require a specific value for the limit.

Assumption 4
.

Assume either one of the following:

i)

\tau>0
in the penalty of estimator (
6
), and let
\tau^{\prime}=\tau/\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
.

ii)

\tau=0
and for
c>0
,
P(U_{1})\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}
and
P(U_{1})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
, where
U_{1}=\{\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\rVert_{0}\leq n(1-c)/2\}
is the event that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
has at most
n(1-c)/2
nonzero rows. Finally,
T\leq e^{\sqrt{n}}
.

4
(i) requires that the Ridge penalty in (
6
) be enforced,
so that the objective function is strongly convex.

4
(ii), on the other hand, does not require strong convexity
but that the number of nonzero rows of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is small enough with high-probability,
which is a reasonable assumption when the tuning parameter
\lambda
in (
6
) is large enough and
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is sparse enough. While we do not prove
in the present paper that
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{1})\to 1
under assumptions on the tuning parameter
\lambda
and the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, results of a similar nature have been obtained previously in several group-Lasso settings

[
28
, Theorem 3.1]
,

[
27
, Lemma 6]
,

[
5
, Lemma C.3]
,

[
3
, Proposition 3.7]
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose that
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold for all
n,p
as
n\to\infty
, then almost surely

\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)({\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq\Theta_{1}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n+\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\trace(\boldsymbol{S})\big)

(15)

for some non-negative random variable
\Theta_{1}
of constant order, in the sense that
{\mathbb{E}}[\Theta_{1}^{2}]\leq C(\tau^{\prime})(T\wedge(1+\frac{p}{n}))(1+\frac{p}{n}))\leq C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})

under
4
(i),
and
{{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\Theta_{1}^{2}]}\leq C(\gamma,c)
under
4
(ii), where
I(\Omega)
is the indicator function of an event
\Omega

with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega)\to 1
.

Above,
\Theta_{1}\geq 0
is said to be of constant order because
\Theta_{1}=O_{P}(1)

follows from
{\mathbb{E}}[\Theta_{1}^{2}]\leq C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
or from
{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\Theta_{1}^{2}]\leq C(\gamma,c)
if the stochastically bounded notation
O_{P}(1)
is allowed to
hide constants depending on
(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
or
(\gamma,c)
only.
In the left-hand side of (
15
), multiplication by
\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
on both sides of the error
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}
can be further removed, as

\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)({\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)\rVert_{\rm{F}}\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}^{2}

(16)

and the fact that
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
is bounded from above with high probability by a constant depending on
\gamma,\tau^{\prime},c
only.
Upper bounds on
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
are formally stated in the supplementary material.

3.3
Understanding the right-hand side of (
15
), and the multi-task generalization error

Before coming back to upper bounds on the error
\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
,
let us study the quantities appearing in the right-hand side of (
15
). By (
9
),
\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n

is the mean squared norm of the residuals and is observable, while the squared error

\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
and
\trace[\boldsymbol{S}]
are unknown.
By analogy with single task models, we define the
generalization error
as the matrix
\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}+\boldsymbol{S}
of size
T\times T
, whose
(t,t^{\prime})
-th entry is

{\mathbb{E}}[(Y^{new}_{t}-\boldsymbol{x}_{new}^{T}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t})(Y^{new}_{t^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{x}_{new}^{T}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}})|(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})]

where
(Y^{new}_{t},Y^{new}_{t^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{x}_{new})
is independent of
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})

and has the same distribution
as
(Y_{it},Y_{it^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{x}_{i})
for some
i=1,...,n
.
Estimating the generalization error is useful for parameter tuning:
since

\trace[\boldsymbol{H}^{T}\boldsymbol{H}+\boldsymbol{S}]=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\trace[\boldsymbol{S}],

(17)

minimizing an estimator of
\trace[\boldsymbol{H}^{T}\boldsymbol{H}+\boldsymbol{S}]
is a useful proxy
to minimize the Frobenius error
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}

of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
.
The following theorem gives an estimate for the generalization error matrix
as well as a consistent estimator for its trace (
17
).

Theorem 3.4

(Generalization error)
.

Let
1
,
4
,
2
and
3
be fulfilled. Then

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}/n-(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)(\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}+\boldsymbol{S})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq\Theta_{2}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n+\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\trace(\boldsymbol{S})\big),

for some non-negative random variable
\Theta_{2}
of constant order, in the sense that
{\mathbb{E}}[\Theta_{2}]\leq C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
under
4
(i),
and with
{{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\Theta_{2}]}\leq C(\gamma,c)
under
4
(ii), where
I(\Omega)
is the indicator function of an event
\Omega

with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega)\to 1
.

Furthermore, if
T=o(n)
as
n,p\to\infty
while
\tau^{\prime},\gamma,c
stay constant,
then

\frac{\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}}{\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n}\overset{p}{\to}1.

(18)

In the above theorem,
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{H}
are unknown, while
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
and
\boldsymbol{F}
can be computed from the observed data
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})
.
Thus (
18
) shows that
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n
is a consistent estimate for
the unobserved quantity
\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
.

3.4
Back to bounds on
\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}

We are now ready to present our main result on the error bounds for
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
.
It is a consequence of (
15
), (
16
) and (
18
).

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
1
,
4
,
2
and
3
be fulfilled and
T=o(n)
. Then

\displaystyle\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq O_{P}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n),

(19)

\displaystyle\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq O_{P}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}].

(20)

Here the
O_{P}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
notation involves constants depending on
\gamma,\tau^{\prime},c
.

It is instructive at this point to compare (
20
)
with the lower bound (
14
) on the Frobenius error
of the method-of-moments estimator. When
p\geq n
then
{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\geq\frac{c}{n}[\trace[\boldsymbol{S}]+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]^{2}
;
this is the situation where the Statistician does not attempt to estimate

\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, and pays a price of
[\trace[\boldsymbol{S}]+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]^{2}/n
.
On the other hand, by definition of
\boldsymbol{H}
in (
9
), the right-hand side of (
20
),
when squared, is of order

n^{-1}[\trace[\boldsymbol{S}]+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]^{2}
.
Here the error bound only depends on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
through
the estimation error for the nuisance
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
.
This explains that when
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is a good estimator of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

and
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
is smaller compared to
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
, the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
that leverages
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
will outperform
the method-of-moments estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
which does not attempt to estimate the nuisance.

Finally, the next results show that under additional assumptions,
the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
enjoys Frobenius error bounds similar to the oracle estimator
\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
.

Assumption 5
.

\text{SNR }\leq\mathfrak{snr}
for some positive constant
\mathfrak{snr}
independent of
n,p,T
, where
\text{SNR}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/\trace(\boldsymbol{S})
denotes the signal-to-noise ratio of the multi-task linear model (
1
).

Corollary 3.6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
(i),
5
and
T=o(n)
hold, then

\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq O_{P}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})\trace(\boldsymbol{S}),

(21)

where
O_{P}(\cdot)
hides constants depending on
\gamma,\tau^{\prime},\mathfrak{snr}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq O_{P}(T/n)\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}=o_{P}(1)\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2},

\displaystyle\big|\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}\rVert_{*}-\trace(\boldsymbol{S})\big|\leq O_{P}(\sqrt{T/n})\trace(\boldsymbol{S})=o_{P}(1)\trace(\boldsymbol{S}).

Corollary 3.7
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
(ii)
and
T=o(n)
hold.
If
\lVert\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\rVert_{0}\leq(1-c)n/2
and the tuning parameter
\lambda
is of the form
\lambda=\mu\sqrt{\trace(\boldsymbol{S})/n}
for some positive constant
\mu
,
then

\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq O_{P}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})(1+\mu^{2})\trace(\boldsymbol{S}),

(22)

where
O_{P}(\cdot)
hides constants depending on
c,\gamma,\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.

Comparing
Corollaries

3.6
and
3.7
with
Proposition

3.1
, we conclude
that
\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
is of the same order as the Frobenius error of the oracle estimator
in (
12
) up to constants depending on the signal-to-noise ratio,
\gamma
, and
\tau^{\prime}
under
4
(i), and up to constants depending on
\mu
,
c,\gamma,\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

under
4
(ii).

The error bounds in (
20
)-(
22
) are measured in Frobenius norm, similarly to existing works on
noise covariance estimation
[
30
]
. Outside the context of linear regression models, much work has been devoted to covariance estimation
in the operator norm.
By the loose bound
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{M}\right\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{M}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}
, our upper bounds carry over to the operator norm.
The same cannot be said for lower bounds, since for instance

{\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(oracle)}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{op}}^{2}\big]\asymp n^{-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\trace(\boldsymbol{S})
(see, e.g.,
[
25
, Corollary 2]
).

(a)
Boxplot for estimating a
full rank

\boldsymbol{S}

(b)
Boxplot for estimating a
low rank

\boldsymbol{S}

Figure 1:
Boxplots for Frobenius norm loss over 100 repetitions.

4
Numerical experiments

Regarding parameters for our simulations,
we set
T=20
,
p=1.5n
and
n
equals successively
1000,1500,2000
.
We consider two settings for the noise covariance matrix:

\boldsymbol{S}
is full-rank and
\boldsymbol{S}
is low-rank.
The complete construction of
\boldsymbol{S}
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{X}
,
as well as implementation details are given in the supplementary material.

We compare our proposed estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
with relevant estimators including
(1) the naive estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\text{(naive)}}=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}
,
(2) the method-of-moments estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
defined in Proposition
3.2
, and
(3) the oracle estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(oracle)}}=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}
.
The performance of each estimator is measured in Frobenius norm: for instance,
\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
is the loss for proposed estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
.

Figure

1
displays the boxplots of the Frobenius loss from the different methods over 100 repetitions.

Figure

1
shows that, besides the oracle estimator, our proposed estimator has the best performance with significantly smaller loss compared to the naive and method-of-moments estimators.

Since the estimation target is a
T\times T
matrix, we also want to compare different estimators in terms of the bias and standard deviation for each entry of
\boldsymbol{S}
.

Figure

2
presents the heatmaps of bias and standard deviation from different estimators for full rank
\boldsymbol{S}
with
n=1000
.
The remaining heatmaps for different
n
and for estimation of low rank
\boldsymbol{S}
are available in the supplementary material.

As expected, the oracle estimator has best performance in
Figure

1

and smallest bias and variance in
Figure

2
.
The naive estimator has large bias as we see in
Figure

2
,
though it has small standard deviation.
The method-of-moments estimator is unbiased but its variance is relatively large,
which means its performance is not stable, as was reflected in
Figure

1
.
Our proposed estimator improves on both the naive and method-of-moments estimators
because it has much smaller bias than the former,
while having smaller standard deviation than the latter.

Heatmap of
bias
for each entry

(b)
Heatmap of
standard deviation
for each entry

Figure 2:
Heatmaps for estimation of full rank
\boldsymbol{S}
with
n=1000
over 100 repetitions.

5
Limitations and future work

One limitation of the proposed estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
is
that its construction necessitates the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
Let us first mention that the estimator

n^{-1}\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
of
\trace(\boldsymbol{S})+\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
in
Theorem

3.4
does not require knowing
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. Thus, this estimator can further be used as a proxy of the error
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}
,
say for parameter tuning, without the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
The problem of estimating
\boldsymbol{S}
with known

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
was studied in
[
15
]
for
T=2
: in this
inaccurate covariate model
and for
p/n\leq\gamma
, our results yield the convergence rate
n^{-1/2}

for
\boldsymbol{S}
which improves upon the rate
n^{-c_{0}}
for a non-explicit constant
c_{0}>0
in
[
15
, Theorem 2.1]
.

In order to use
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is unknown, one may plug-in
an estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
in
Equation

11
, resulting in an extra term
of order
\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\rVert_{\rm{F}}
for the Frobenius error. See
[
17
, §4]
for related discussions in the
T=1
(single-task) case. While, under the proportional regime
p/n\to\gamma
,
no estimator is consistent for all covariance matrices
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
in operator norm, consistent estimators do exist under additional structural assumptions
[
12
,
20
,
14
]
.
If available, additional unlabeled samples
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i})_{i\geq n+1}
can also
be used to construct norm-consistent estimator of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.

Future directions include extending estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
to utilize
other estimators of the nuisance
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

than the multi-task elastic-net (
6
); for instance
(
7
) or the estimators studied in
[
36
,
30
,
11
]
. In the simpler case where
columns of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
are estimated independently on each task, e.g.,
if the
T
columns of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
are Lasso estimators

(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(t)})_{t\in[T]}
each computed from
\boldsymbol{y}{}^{(t)}
,
then minor modifications of our proof yield that the estimator (
11
)
with
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}=\diag(\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(1)}\|_{0},...,\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(T)}\|_{0})
enjoys similar
Frobenius norm bounds of order
n^{-1/2}
.

SUPPLEMENT

This supplement is organized as follows:

•

In
Appendix

A
we provide details regarding the setting of our simulations, as well as additional experiment results.

•

In
Appendix

B
we establish an upper bound for the out-of-sample error, which we could not put in the full paper due to page length limit.

•

Appendix

C
provides the upper bound for
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
mentioned after
Theorem

3.3
in the full paper, and
Appendices
D
,
E
,
F
contain preliminary theoretical statements, which will be useful for proving our main results in the full paper.

•

Appendix

G
contains proofs of main results in
Section

3
of the full paper and
Appendix

B
.

•

Appendix

H
contains proofs of preliminary results in Appendices
C
to
F
.

Notation.

Here we introduce basic notations that will be used throughout this supplement.
We use indexes
i
and
l
only to loop or sum over
[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, use
j
and
k
only to loop or sum over
[p]=\{1,2,\ldots,p\}
,
use
t
and
t^{\prime}
only to loop or sum over
[T]=\{1,2,\ldots,T\}
, so that
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
(and
\boldsymbol{e}_{l}
) refer to the
i
-th (and
l
-th) canonical basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
(and
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
) refer to the
j
-th (and
k
-th) canonical basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
(and
\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}
) refer to the
t
-th (and
t^{\prime}
-th) canonical basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
.
For any two real numbers
a
and
b
, let
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
, and
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
.
Positive constants that depend on
\gamma,\tau^{\prime}
only are denoted by
C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
, and positive constants that depend on
\gamma,c
only are denoted by
C(\gamma,c)
. The values of these constants
may vary from place to place.

Appendix A
Experiment details and additional simulation results

A.1
Experimental details

This section provides more experimental detail for
Section

4
of the full paper.

We consider two types of noise covariance matrix:
(i)
\boldsymbol{S}
is full-rank with
(t,t^{\prime})
-th entry
\boldsymbol{S}_{t,t^{\prime}}=\frac{\cos(t-t^{\prime})}{1+\sqrt{|t-t^{\prime}|}}
;
(ii)
\boldsymbol{S}
is low-rank with
\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}
, where
\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times 10}
has i.i.d. entries from
{\cal N}(0,1/T)
.

To build the coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, we first set its sparsity pattern, i.e., we define the support
{S}
of cardinality
|{S}|=0.1p
,
then we generate an intermediate matrix
\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}
.
The
j
-th row of
\boldsymbol{B}
is sampled from
{\cal N}_{T}(\boldsymbol{0},p^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{T})
if
j\in{S}
,
otherwise we set it to be the zero vector.
Finally we let
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}=\boldsymbol{B}[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})/\trace(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{B})]^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
which forces a signal-to-noise ratio of exactly
1
.

The design matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
is constructed by independently sampling its rows from
{\cal N}_{p}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jk}=|j-k|^{0.5}
.

The Python library Scikit-learn
[
32
]
is used to calculate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
6
).
More precisely we invoke
MultiTaskElasticNetCV
to
obtain
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
by 5-fold cross-validation with parameters
l1-ratio=[0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1]
,
n_alpha=100
.
To compute the interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}

we used the efficient implementation described in
[
5
, Section 5]
.

The full code needed to reproduce our experiments is part of the supplementary material.
A detailed Readme file is located in the corresponding folder.

The simulations results reported in the full paper and this supplementary material were run on a cluster of 50 CPU-cores (each is an Intel Xeon E5-2680 v4 @2.40GHz) equipped with a total of 150 GB of RAM.
It takes approximately six hours to obtain all of our simulation results.

Appendix B
Out-of-sample error estimation

In this appendix, we present a by-product of our techniques for estimating the noise covariance.
Suppose we wish to evaluate the performance of a regression method on a new data, we define the out-of-sample error for the multi-task linear model (
1
) as

{\mathbb{E}}\big[({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\text{new}}\boldsymbol{x}_{\text{new}}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})|(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})\big]=\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H},

where
\boldsymbol{x}_{\text{new}}
is independent of the data
(\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{Y})
with the same distribution as any row of
\boldsymbol{X}
.

The following theorem on estimation of out-of-sample error is an by-product of our technique for constructing
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}
.

Theorem B.1

(Out-of-sample error)
.

Under the same conditions of
Theorem

3.3
, with
\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}+\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}-p\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\big)\rVert_{\rm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Theta_{3}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n)

for some non-negative random variable
\Theta_{3}
of constant order, in the sense that
{\mathbb{E}}[\Theta_{3}]\leq C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
under
4
(i),
and with
{{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\Theta_{3}]}\leq C(\gamma,c)
under
4
(ii), where
I(\Omega)
is the indicator function of an event
\Omega

with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega)\to 1
.

Theorem

B.1
generalizes the result in
[
4
]
to multi-task setting.
While the out-of-sample error
\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}
is unknown, the quantities
\boldsymbol{Z}
,
\boldsymbol{F}
,
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
are observable. Since typically the quantity
(\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n)
is of a constant order,
Theorem

B.1
suggests the following estimate of
\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}
:

\frac{1}{n^{2}}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\big(\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}+\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}-p\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\big)(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1},

which can further be used for parameter tuning in multi-task linear model.

We present the proof of
Theorem

B.1
in
Section

G.8

Appendix C
Useful operator norm bounds

Let us first introduce two events besides the event
U_{1}=\big\{\lVert{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\rVert_{0}\leq n(1-c)/2\big\}
in
4
(ii), we define events
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
as below,

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=\big\{\inf_{\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq(1-c)n}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{b}\|^{2}/(n\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{b}\|^{2})>\eta\big\},

\displaystyle U_{3}

\displaystyle=\big\{\lVert\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\rm{op}}<2\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}\big\}.

Under
2
and
3
,
[
7
, Lemma B.1]
guarantees
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{2})\geq 1-C(\gamma,c)e^{-C(\gamma,c)n}
for some constant
\eta
that only depends on constants
\gamma,c
.
Under
2
,
[
16
, Theorem II.13]
guarantees
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{3})>1-e^{-n/2}
and there exists a random variable
z\sim{\cal N}(0,1)
s.t.
\lVert\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}+z
almost surely. Therefore, under
2
and
3
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\rm{op}}^{2}]\leq(1+\sqrt{p/n})^{2}+n^{-1}\leq C(\gamma).

(23)

Furthermore, under
3
,
2
and
4
(ii),
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3})\to 1
by a union bound, and for large enough
n
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big\{(U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3})^{c}\big\}<

\displaystyle 1/T+C(\gamma,c)e^{-n/C(\gamma,c)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}(1+TC(\gamma,c)e^{-n/C(\gamma,c)})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}(1+C(\gamma,c)e^{\sqrt{n}-n/C(\gamma,c)})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}C(\gamma,c).

(24)

Now we provide the operator norm bounds for
\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
and
(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}
.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose that
2
holds. If
\tau>0
in (
6
) with
\tau^{\prime}=\tau/\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
, then

(i)

\lVert\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq 1
.

(ii)

In the event
U_{3}
, we have
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq 1+(\tau^{\prime})^{-1}(2+\sqrt{p/n})^{2}
. Furthermore,
{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}]\leq 1+(\tau^{\prime})^{-1}[(1+\sqrt{p/n})^{2}+n^{-1}].

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose that
2
holds.
If
\tau=0
in (
6
), then

(i)

In the event
U_{1}
, we have
\lVert\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq 1
.

(ii)

In the event
U_{1}
,
\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq C(c)
. Hence,
{\mathbb{E}}[I(U_{1})\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\rVert_{\rm{op}}]\leq C(c).

Lemma C.3
.

With
\boldsymbol{N}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}^{\top})
, we have
\lVert\boldsymbol{N}\rVert_{\rm{op}}\leq 1
.

Appendix D
Lipschitz and differential properties for a given, fixed noise matrix
\boldsymbol{E}

We need to study Lipschitz and differential properties of certain mappings when the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{E}
is fixed.
Let

g:{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

g(\boldsymbol{B})=\tau\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/2+\lambda\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\rVert_{2,1}
be the penalty in
(
6
).
For a fixed value of
\boldsymbol{E}
,
define the mappings

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{Z})=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\frac{1}{2n}\lVert\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{Z}\bar{\boldsymbol{H}}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+g(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\bar{\boldsymbol{H}})

\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T})

(25)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{Z})=\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{Z})

\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T})

(26)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}

\displaystyle\longmapsto D(\boldsymbol{Z})=(\lVert\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{Z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\lVert\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{Z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n)^{1/2}

\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}\to{\mathbb{R}}).

(27)

Next, define the random variable
\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, and let us use the convention that if arguments of
\boldsymbol{H},\boldsymbol{F}
or
D
are omitted then these mappings are implicitly taken at the realized value of the random variable
\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
where
\boldsymbol{X}
is the observed design matrix. With this convention and by definition of the above mappings, we then have

\boldsymbol{H}=\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{Z})=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
as well as

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{Z})=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}

and
D=[\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n]^{1/2}

so that the notation is consistent with the rest of the paper
(in particular, with (
9
)).

Finally, denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{Z}
by
z_{ij}

throughout this appendix, and the corresponding partial derivatives of the above mappings
by
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{ij}}
.

D.1
Lipschitz properties

Lemma D.1
.

For multi-task elastic-net (i.e.,
\tau>0
in (
6
)), the mapping
\boldsymbol{Z}\mapsto D^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}/\sqrt{n}
is
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}L
-Lipschitz with
L=8\max(1,(2\tau^{\prime})^{-1})
, where
\tau^{\prime}=\tau/\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
.

Lemma D.2
.

For multi-task group Lasso (i.e.,
\tau=0
in (
6
)).
we have

(1)

In
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, the map
\boldsymbol{Z}\mapsto D^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}/\sqrt{n}
is
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}L
-Lipschitz with
L=8\max(1,(2\eta)^{-1})
.

(2)

In
U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3}
, the map
\boldsymbol{Z}\mapsto D^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}/n
is
n^{-1/2}(1+(2+\sqrt{p/n})L)
-Lipschitz, where
L=8\max(1,(2\eta)^{-1})
as in (1).

Corollary D.3
.

Suppose that
4
holds, then

(1)

Under
4
(i) that
\tau>0
and
\tau^{\prime}=\tau/\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{ij}\Big(\frac{\partial D}{\partial z_{ij}}\Big)^{2}\leq n^{-1}D^{2}[4\max(1,(2\tau^{\prime})^{-1})]^{2}.

This implies that

nD^{-2}\sum_{ij}(\frac{\partial D}{\partial z_{ij}})^{2}\leq C(\tau^{\prime}).

(2)

Under
4
(ii) that
\tau=0
and
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{1})\to 1
, in the event
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{ij}\Big(\frac{\partial D}{\partial z_{ij}}\Big)^{2}\leq n^{-1}D^{2}[4\max(1,(2\eta)^{-1})]^{2}.

This implies that

nD^{-2}\sum_{ij}(\frac{\partial D}{\partial z_{ij}})^{2}\leq C(\eta)=C(\gamma,c)
since
\eta
is a constant that only depends on
\gamma,c
.

D.2
Derivative formulae

Note that with a fixed noise
\boldsymbol{E}
,
Lemmas

D.1
and
D.2
guarantee that the map
\boldsymbol{Z}\mapsto\boldsymbol{F}
is Lipschitz, hence the derivative exists almost everywhere by Rademacher’s theorem.
We present the formula for derivative of this map in
Lemma

D.4
.

Lemma D.4
.

Recall
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
with
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
defined in (
6
). Under
4
(i)
\tau>0
, or under
4
(ii)
\tau=0
and in the event
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, for each
i,l\in[n],j\in[p],t\in[T]
, the following derivative exists almost everywhere and has the expression

\frac{\partial F_{lt}}{\partial z_{ij}}=D_{ij}^{lt}+\Delta_{ij}^{lt},

where

D_{ij}^{lt}=-(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top})(\boldsymbol{I}_{nT}-\boldsymbol{N})(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{l}),
and

\Delta_{ij}^{lt}=-(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{l}^{\top})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}})\bigl(\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\bigr)(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}).

Furthermore, a straightforward calculation yields

\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{ij}^{it}=-\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}.

Lemma D.5
.

Suppose that
4
holds.

(1)

Under
4
(i) that
\tau>0
and
\tau^{\prime}=\tau/\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rVert_{\rm{op}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{ij}\left\lVert\frac{\partial(\boldsymbol{F}/D)}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert^{2}_{\rm F}\leq\underbrace{4\max(1,(\tau^{\prime})^{-1}(T\wedge\frac{p}{n}))+2n^{-1}[4\max(1,(2\tau^{\prime})^{-1})]^{2}}_{f(\tau^{\prime},T,n,p)}.

(2)

Under
4
(ii) that
\tau=0
and
{\mathbb{P}}(U_{1})\to 1
, in the event
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{ij}\left\lVert\frac{\partial(\boldsymbol{F}/D)}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert^{2}_{\rm F}\leq\underbrace{4\max(1,(\eta)^{-1}(T\wedge\frac{p}{n}))+2n^{-1}[4\max(1,(2\eta)^{-1})]^{2}}_{f(\eta,T,n,p)}.

Furthermore, the right-hand side in (1) can be bounded from above by
C(\tau^{\prime})(T\wedge\frac{p}{n})
, and the right-hand side in (2) can be bounded from above by
C(\gamma,c)
in the regime
p/n\leq\gamma
.

Appendix E
Lipschitz and differential properties for a given, fixed design matrix

We also need to study Lipschitz and derivative properties of functions
of the noise
\boldsymbol{E}
when the design
\boldsymbol{X}
is fixed. Formally, for a given and fixed design matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
, define the function

\boldsymbol{E}\mapsto\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{E})
by
the value
\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
of the residual matrix
when the observed data
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})
is
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E})

and with
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
the estimator (
6
).
Note that this map is 1-Lipschitz by
[
6
, proposition 3]
. Rademacher’s theorem thus guarantees this map is differentiable almost everywhere. We denote its partial derivative by
\frac{\partial}{\partial E_{it}}

for each entry
(E_{it})_{i\in[n],t\in[T]}
of the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{E}
.
We present its derivative formula in
Lemma

E.1
below.

Lemma E.1
.

For each
i,l\in[n],t,t^{\prime}\in[T]
, the following derivative exists almost everywhere and has the expression

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{lt}}{\partial E_{it^{\prime}}}=\boldsymbol{e}_{l}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{e}_{l}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}^{\top})\boldsymbol{e}_{i}.

As a consequence, we further have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial F_{it}}{\partial E_{it^{\prime}}}=\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}},\quad\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}}{\partial E_{it^{\prime}}}=\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}.

Appendix F
Probabilistic tools

We first list some useful variants of Stein’s formulae and Gaussian-Poincaré inequalities. Let
f^{\prime}
denote the derivative of a differentiable univariate function. For a differentiable vector-valued function
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, denote its Jacobian (derivative) and divergence respectively by
\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})
and
\div\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})
, i.e.,
[\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})]_{i,l}=\frac{\partial f_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})}{\partial z_{l}}
for
i,l\in[n]
, and
\div\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})=\trace(\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}))
.

Lemma F.1

(Second-order Stein’s formula
[
8
]
)
.

The following identities hold provided the involved derivatives exist a.e. and the expectations are finite.

i)

z\sim{\cal N}(0,1)
,
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, then

{\mathbb{E}}[(zf(z)-f^{\prime}(z))^{2}]={\mathbb{E}}[f(z)^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[(f^{\prime}(z))^{2}].

ii)

\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\cal N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
,
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then

{\mathbb{E}}[(\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})-\div\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}))^{2}]={\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert^{2}+\trace[(\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}))^{2}]\big]\leq{\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert^{2}+\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\big],

where the inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

iii)

More generally, for
\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\cal N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
\boldsymbol{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[(\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})-\trace(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}))^{2}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert^{2}+\trace[(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z}))^{2}]\big]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\big[\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert^{2}+\lVert(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\big],

where the inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Lemma F.2

(Gaussian-Poincaré inequality
[
13
]
)
.

The following inequalities hold provided the right-hand side derivatives exist a.e. and the expectations are finite.

i)

z\sim{\cal N}(0,1)
,
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, then

\text{Var}[f(z)]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[(f^{\prime}(z))^{2}].

ii)

\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\cal N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
,
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, then

\text{Var}[f(\boldsymbol{z})]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert^{2}].

iii)

\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\cal N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{n})
,
\boldsymbol{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, then

{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})-{\mathbb{E}}[\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})]\rVert^{2}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}].

iv)

More generally, for
\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\cal N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
\boldsymbol{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, then

{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})-{\mathbb{E}}[\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})]\rVert^{2}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}].

Now we present a few important lemmas, whose proofs are based on
Lemma

F.1
and
Lemma

F.2
.

Lemma F.3
.

Assume that Assumption
1
holds. For fixed
\boldsymbol{X}
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\lVert\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}/{\tilde{D}}-\boldsymbol{S}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})/{\tilde{D}}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\Bigr]\leq 4\trace(\boldsymbol{S}),

where
{\tilde{D}}=\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+n\trace(\boldsymbol{S})\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Lemma F.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
be two locally Lipschitz functions of
\boldsymbol{Z}
with i.i.d.
{\cal N}(0,1)
entries, then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big\lVert\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{V}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{ij}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}\Bigr)\Big\rVert_{\rm F}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{U}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\sum_{ij}\Big[2\left\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{U}}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+2\left\lVert\boldsymbol{U}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{V}}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\Big].

Corollary F.5
.

Assume the same setting as
Lemma

F.4
.
If on some open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega^{c})\leq C/T
for some constant
C
, we have (i)
\boldsymbol{U}
is
n^{-1/2}L_{1}
-Lipschitz and
\lVert\boldsymbol{U}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq 1
, (ii)
\boldsymbol{V}
is
n^{-1/2}L_{2}
-Lipschitz and
\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq K
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[I(\Omega)\Big\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{V}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{ij}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}\Bigr)\Big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle K^{2}+2C(K^{2}L_{1}^{2}+L_{2}^{2})+2{\mathbb{E}}\Big[I(\Omega)\sum_{ij}\Big(K^{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{U}}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\left\lVert\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{V}}{\partial z_{ij}}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\Big)\Big].

Lemma F.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}

be two locally Lipschitz
functions of
\boldsymbol{Z}
with i.i.d.
{\cal N}(0,1)
entries.
Assume also that
\lVert\boldsymbol{U}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\vee\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\rVert_{\rm{F}}\leq 1
almost surely.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\Big\lVert p\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}-\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{ij}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}-\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{Z}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}\Bigr)\Big\rVert_{\rm{F}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\partial}\|\boldsymbol{V}\|_{\partial}+\sqrt{p}\bigl(\sqrt{2}+(3+\sqrt{2})(\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\partial}+\|\boldsymbol{V}\|_{\partial})\bigr),

where
\partial_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\partial\boldsymbol{U}/\partial z_{ij}
,
and
\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\partial}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{\mathbb{E}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lVert\partial_{ij}\boldsymbol{U}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proposition F.7
.

Suppose that
1
holds.

Let
\boldsymbol{Q}_{1}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\big(\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{S}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\big)}{\lVert\boldsymbol{S}^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\rm{F}}(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n+\trace(\boldsymbol{S}))^{\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}
,
then
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}_{1}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\leq 4
.

Proposition F.8
.

Suppose that
2
,
3
and
4
hold.
Let

\boldsymbol{Q}_{2}=\frac{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}(p\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})+(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}\big)}{(\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n)n^{-\frac{1}{2}}},

then
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}_{2}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\leq C(\tau^{\prime})(T\wedge(1+\frac{p}{n}))(1+\frac{p}{n})
under
4
(i), and

{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}_{2}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}]\leq C(\gamma,c)
under
4
(ii) for some set
\Omega
with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega)\to 1
.

Proposition F.9
.

Suppose that
2
,
3
and
4
hold.
Let
\Xi=(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}
, and

\boldsymbol{Q}_{3}=\frac{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\big(p\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}-(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\boldsymbol{H}^{\top}\boldsymbol{H}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})-\Xi-\Xi^{\top}\big)}{(\lVert\boldsymbol{H}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{F}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}/n)n^{-\frac{1}{2}}},

then
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}_{3}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}]\leq C(\gamma,\tau^{\prime})
under
4
(i), and
{\mathbb{E}}[I(\Omega)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}_{3}\right\rVert_{\rm{F}}]\leq C(\gamma,c)
under
4
(ii) for some set
\Omega
with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega)\to 1
.

Appendix G
Proofs of main results

In this appendix, we provide proofs of the theoretical results in
Section

3
of the pull paper and
Appendix

B
of this supplement.

G.1
Proof of Proposition
3.1

Proof of
Proposition

3.1
.

With
\boldsymbol{S}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{T}

the spectral decomposition of
\boldsymbol{S}
,
we have
\lVert\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-n\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}=\sum_{t\in[T]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[T]}[\boldsymbol{u}_{t^{\prime}}^{T}((\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-n\boldsymbol{S})\boldsymbol{u}_{t}]^{2}
.
We now compute the expectation of one term
indexed by
(t,t^{\prime})
.
The random variable
\boldsymbol{u}_{t^{\prime}}^{T}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-n\boldsymbol{S})\boldsymbol{u}_{t}

is the sum of
n
i.i.d. mean zero random variables
with the same distribution as
z_{t^{\prime}}z_{t}-\boldsymbol{u}_{t^{\prime}}^{T}\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}

where
(z_{t},z_{t^{\prime}})\sim{\cal N}_{2}(\mathbf{0},\diag(\sigma_{t}^{2},\sigma_{t^{\prime}}^{2}))
. Thus

{\mathbb{E}}[(\boldsymbol{u}_{t^{\prime}}^{T}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}-n\boldsymbol{S})\boldsymbol{u}_{t})^{2}]=n\text{Var}[z_{t^{\prime}}z_{t}-\boldsymbol{u}_{t^{\prime}}^{T}\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}]=n2\sigma_{t}^{4}I_{t=t^{\prime}}+n\sigma_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t^{\prime}}^{2}I_{t\neq t^{\prime}}

due to
\text{Var}[\chi^{2}_{1}]=2

if
t=t^{\prime}
and independence if
t\neq t^{\prime}
.
Summing over all
(t,t^{\prime})\in[T]\times[T]

yields

2n\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}^{4}+n\sum_{t\neq t^{\prime}}\sigma_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t^{\prime}}^{2}=n\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}^{4}+n(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}^{2})^{2}=n\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}+n[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})]^{2}

as desired.

The inequality simply follows from

\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq[\trace(\boldsymbol{S})]^{2}
since
\boldsymbol{S}
is positive semi-definite.

\blacksquare

G.2
Proof of Proposition
3.2

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Without of loss of generality, we assume
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
. For general positive definite
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, the proof follows by replacing
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
with
(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
.

We first derive the method-of-moments estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{\rm{(mm)}}
. Under
1
and
2
with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
,
\boldsymbol{X}
has i.i.d. rows from
{\cal N}_{p}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{p})
,
\boldsymbol{E}
has i.i.d. rows from
{\cal N}_{T}(\bf 0,\boldsymbol{S})
, and
\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{E}
are independent. Then, the expectations of
\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}
are given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big[(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E})\big]=n(\boldsymbol{B}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{S}),

(28)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big[(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E})\big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*})+{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{B}^{*\top}{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E})

\displaystyle=n(n+p+1)\boldsymbol{B}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+np\boldsymbol{S},

(29)

where the last line uses

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top})\sum_{l=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{l}\boldsymbol{x}_{l}^{\top})\Big]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq l}{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{l}\boldsymbol{x}_{l}^{\top})+\sum_{i=l}{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{l}\boldsymbol{x}_{l}^{\top})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle n(n-1)\boldsymbol{I}_{p}^{2}+n{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle n(n-1)\boldsymbol{I}_{p}+n[2\boldsymbol{I}_{p}^{2}+\trace(\boldsymbol{I}_{p})\boldsymbol{I}_{p}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle n(n+p+1)\boldsymbol{I}_{p},

and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E})={\mathbb{E}}\big[{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}|\boldsymbol{E})\big]={\mathbb{E}}\big[\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top})\boldsymbol{E}\big]=np\boldsymbol{S}.

Solving for
\boldsymbol{S}
